It's mix shift, not genuine improvement — and within-campaign CTR actually *declined* in most campaigns.

**Overall CTR:** 0.78% (Jun 19–Jul 5) → 0.95% (Jul 6–Jul 22), +0.166pp (+21% relative).

**Per-campaign, period A → B:**

| Campaign | Type | Impr. share A→B | CTR A→B |
|---|---|---|---|
| Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 | Video (remarketing) | 88.0% → 70.9% | 0.257% → 0.165% (↓36%) |
| Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US | Search | 5.1% → 5.7% | 7.97% → 8.26% (↑4%) |
| Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US | Search | 4.1% → 12.3% | 1.28% → 0.95% (↓25%) |
| Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US | Search | 2.6% → 9.2% | 2.70% → 2.15% (↓20%) |
| Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US | Search | 0.2% → 1.9% | 12.5% → 2.53% (↓80%) |

**What's driving it:** The low-CTR YT video remarketing campaign shrank from 88% to 71% of impressions, while the higher-CTR Search campaigns picked up share (JobSearchTools and DirectSwitchers roughly tripled their share). That's the entire story of the CTR "improvement" — blending toward Search inflates the average even though Search itself got worse.

**Decomposition of the +0.166pp change** (holding weights/rates fixed in turn):
- **Mix effect** (shift toward Search, at old CTRs): **+0.49pp** — this is the full positive contribution.
- **Rate effect** (actual per-campaign CTR change, at old mix): **−0.11pp** — campaigns got worse on average.
- **Interaction** (the campaigns gaining the most share — JobSearchTools, Telugu — are also the ones whose CTR fell hardest): **−0.21pp**.

Net: +0.49 − 0.11 − 0.21 ≈ +0.17pp, matching the observed change.

**Bottom line:** 4 of 5 campaigns had *lower* CTR in the latest period (Telugu fell 80% relative, off small volume). Only Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US genuinely improved (+4%). The account-level CTR gain is a Simpson's-paradox artifact of Video losing impression share to Search, not real creative/targeting performance improvement — if anything, underlying rates softened.

I'll pull campaign-level CTR data for both periods to decompose the change into rate vs mix effects.

**Bottom line: it's mix shift, not real improvement.** Blended account CTR did rise (0.78% → 0.95%, +0.17pp), but the underlying campaigns mostly got *worse* at converting impressions to clicks — the account just sent a smaller share of impressions through your lowest-CTR campaign.

**Per-campaign CTR, prior (6/19–7/05) vs latest (7/06–7/22):**

| Campaign | Impr. share (prior→latest) | CTR (prior→latest) | Within-campaign Δ |
|---|---|---|---|
| Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 | 88.0% → 71.0% | 0.26% → 0.17% | **−0.09pp** |
| Jul26_Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US | 5.1% → 5.7% | 7.97% → 8.26% | **+0.29pp** (only improver) |
| Jul26_Competitor_JobSearchTools_US | 4.1% → 12.3% | 1.28% → 0.95% | **−0.32pp** |
| Jul26_Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US | 2.6% → 9.2% | 2.70% → 2.15% | **−0.55pp** |
| Jul26_Competitor_Telugu_US | 0.2% → 1.9% | 12.50% → 2.53% | **−9.97pp** (low volume) |

**Decomposition of the +0.17pp account CTR change:**
- **Mix effect (share shift, holding rates fixed): +0.49pp.** The low-CTR YT_RT_v2 video/remarketing campaign shrank from 88% to 71% of impressions, while three moderate/higher-CTR search-competitor campaigns (JobSearchTools, DirectSwitchers, Telugu) each roughly tripled their impression share. Reweighting purely by the new mix (applying old rates) would have pushed blended CTR to ~1.27%.
- **Rate effect (actual within-campaign CTR change, at new mix weights): −0.32pp.** Four of five campaigns clicked *worse* per impression than in the prior window — only NonBrand_US search improved.
- Net: +0.49pp (mix) − 0.32pp (rate) ≈ +0.17pp observed.

**Takeaway:** the "CTR improvement" is an artifact of traffic reallocating away from a naturally low-CTR video/remarketing campaign toward search-style campaigns — not campaigns performing better individually. If anything, engagement quality within campaigns softened (notably DirectSwitchers −0.55pp and JobSearchTools −0.32pp on meaningful volume). I'd flag this in reporting as a composition effect rather than a genuine CTR win, and look at what drove the reallocation (budget/bid changes, YT_RT_v2 delivery constraints, or new campaign ramp-up) before crediting it as performance gain.

## Headline number is misleading — it's a mix shift, not a performance gain

| Period | Impressions | Clicks | Blended CTR |
|---|---|---|---|
| Jun 19 – Jul 5 | 21,222 | 166 | 0.78% |
| Jul 6 – Jul 22 | 29,847 | 283 | 0.95% |
| **Change** | | | **+0.166 pp (+21% relative)** |

**Decomposition of that +0.166pp** (weighted average of Laspeyres/Paasche orderings — sums exactly to the total):
- **Mix effect (impression-share shift): +0.38 pp** — impressions rotated away from the low-CTR YouTube remarketing campaign toward higher-CTR search campaigns.
- **Rate effect (within-campaign CTR change): −0.22 pp** — actual campaign-level CTR performance got *worse*, not better.

## Per-campaign detail

| Campaign | Impr. share (was → now) | CTR (was → now) |
|---|---|---|
| Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 (video remarketing, dominant volume) | 88% → 71% | 0.26% → 0.17% (−36%) |

## Answer

CTR did **not** genuinely improve. Every campaign except NonBrand Search saw its own CTR decline — in some cases sharply (Telugu −80%, DirectSwitchers −20%, JobSearchTools −25%, the core YT remarketing campaign −36%). The blended CTR rose only because volume mix shifted away from the low-CTR YouTube remarketing line (which held 88% of impressions and now holds 71%) toward campaigns that, while themselves declining, still carry a much higher baseline CTR than video remarketing.

Net: the account-level CTR gain is an artifact of budget/impression reallocation, not creative or targeting improvement — underlying rate performance actually deteriorated across the board.

I'll pull the funnel metrics across the benchmark, trend, and current windows, then decompose by device and landing page to localize where the drop-off is happening.

Here's the breakdown. Checkout is **not** the primary problem — the funnel is breaking earlier, at the browse-to-cart stage, and it's being masked by a big influx of low-intent traffic.

## The headline numbers

| Period | Sessions | Browse→Cart | Checkout Completion | Overall Conversion |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2024 (Jan–Jul, baseline) | 523,839 | 4.56% | 39.4% | 1.66% |
| 2025 (Jan–Jul, trend) | 762,536 | 4.07% | 33.0% | 1.42% |
| 2026 (Jan–Jul, current) | 909,372 | **3.08%** | 35.8% | 0.90% |

Traffic is up **+74% vs. the 2024 baseline** (and +19% vs. 2025), but overall conversion has fallen by nearly half. Decomposing it:

- **Browse-to-cart rate has collapsed** — down ~33% relative to 2024 (4.56% → 3.08%), and still falling relative to 2025. This is the dominant driver.
- **Checkout completion is comparatively healthy** — down modestly from 2024 (39.4% → 35.8%) but actually *recovered* from the 2025 dip (33.0% → 35.8%). Checkout is not where the funnel is bleeding.

So: people are landing on the site in much greater numbers, but far fewer of them are engaging enough to add anything to a cart. Once someone does reach checkout, they complete it at close to historical rates.

## Device cut confirms it

| Device | 2026 Sessions | 2026 Browse→Cart | 2024 Browse→Cart | Δ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mobile | 749,364 (82% of traffic) | 2.79% | 4.33% | **-36% relative** |
| Desktop | 134,958 | 4.76% | 5.57% | -15% relative |
| Tablet | 22,277 | 2.78% | 3.97% | -30% relative |

Mobile is where nearly all the traffic growth landed (78%→82% of sessions), and mobile also has the weakest and most-degraded browse-to-cart rate. That combination — more traffic, concentrated on the weakest-engaging device — is arithmetically enough to explain most of the overall conversion decline on its own.

## Landing pages localize it further (last 30 days, 2026-06-30 to 2026-07-30)

Total: 196,738 sessions, 3.12% browse-to-cart, 35.7% checkout completion (consistent with the 2026 aggregate).

The biggest traffic pools are diluting the funnel:

- **`/blogs/heat-stroke-in-dogs/...`** — 11,750 sessions (6% of all traffic), only **1** cart addition, **zero** checkout starts. Pure top-of-funnel content traffic with no shopping intent and no path into the funnel.
- **`/products/pet-monitor`** (41,276 sessions) and **`/products/rv-4g-mini-camera`** (32,231 sessions) — the two largest product pages, together 37% of traffic — convert to cart at only 2.4–2.7%, well below the homepage (5.25%) or `/pages/exclusive-deals` (10.5%, high-intent deal-seekers).
- By contrast, pages with real intent signal — homepage, `/pages/exclusive-deals`, `/pages/waggle-home` — post browse-to-cart rates of 5–10.5% and checkout completion in the 37–43% range, in line with historical health.

## Bottom line

The break is **pre-cart, not at checkout**. Volume is up largely from mobile and content/product-page traffic with weak purchase intent (the blog post alone is a near-zero-conversion pool worth ~6% of sessions), which drags down the blended browse-to-cart rate even though checkout mechanics remain sound. If you want to fix conversion, the lever is engagement/relevance from landing → cart (mobile PDP experience, and whether the blog post has any real path to product), not checkout UX.

*Note: the device-cut total (909,380 sessions) differs from the aggregate query (909,372) by 8 sessions — a minor report-grain reconciliation difference, not something to chase.*

| Device | Sessions 2024 → 2026 | Browse→Cart 2024 → 2026 | Checkout completion 2024 → 2026 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mobile (82% of 2026 traffic) | 409k → 749k (+83%) | 4.33% → **2.79%** (−36% rel.) | 38.8% → 36.6% (−6% rel.) |
| Desktop | 95k → 135k (+42%) | 5.57% → 4.76% (−15% rel.) | 40.8% → **32.8%** (−20% rel.) |

Mobile is where nearly all the new traffic landed, and mobile's browse-to-cart rate is the single biggest drag on the whole funnel — it fell over a third and mobile is 4x desktop's volume. Desktop shows the opposite pattern (a real, if smaller, checkout-completion problem), but it's too small a slice to explain the headline gap.

## Localizing it: current landing pages (Jun 30–Jul 30, 2026)

Site-wide browse-to-cart this window is 3.12% (6,141/196,738). Breaking down the biggest traffic pools:

- **`/blogs/heat-stroke-in-dogs/dog-panting-heat-stroke` — 11,750 sessions, 1 cart-add (0.01%).** This is pure top-of-funnel content traffic with essentially zero purchase intent, and it's large enough (6% of all sessions) to visibly drag down the blended browse-to-cart rate on its own.
- **`/products/pet-monitor` (41,276 sessions) and `/products/rv-4g-mini-camera` (32,231 sessions)** — together 37% of all traffic — convert to cart at 2.71% and 2.42%, well below the site average and far below the homepage (5.25%) or `/pages/exclusive-deals` (10.52%). These are the two biggest volume pools and they're underperforming, not the blog post's problem alone.
- By contrast, checkout completion on these same product pages (39–42%) is close to or above the site average — reinforcing that the leak on high-traffic pages is happening *before* checkout, at the add-to-cart decision.
- The one place checkout genuinely looks broken: **bundle pages** (`/pages/choose-the-bundle` 19.4% completion, `/products/rv-safety-mini-bundle` 24.4%, `/pages/choose-your-bundle` 26.5%) — all well under the ~36% site average. Volume here is modest (~22k sessions combined) but it's a real, isolated checkout-stage issue worth a separate look at the bundle checkout flow.

## Bottom line

The growth in traffic is disproportionately low-intent (a large blog/content pool with near-zero conversion, plus two flagship product pages converting to cart at half the site's best pages), and that's what's swamping the overall conversion rate. Checkout completion has slipped modestly too — concentrated on desktop and on bundle-purchase flows — but it's a secondary issue. Fix priority: (1) qualify or re-target the traffic hitting the blog post and the two big underperforming product pages, (2) separately audit the bundle checkout flow.

*Data note: the 2026 device-level sessions total (909,380) is 8 higher than the account-level total (909,372) — a small report-grain reconciliation difference, immaterial to the conclusions above.*

## Where the funnel is breaking

**Headline:** Completed checkouts actually shrank slightly (8,694 → 8,180, ‑6%) while sessions grew 74% (523,839 → 909,372) vs. the 2024 benchmark. That means overall conversion fell from **1.66% → 0.90%** (‑46% relative). The break is upstream of checkout, not at it.

### Stage decomposition (2024 → 2025 → 2026, YTD Jan–Jul)

| Stage | 2024 | 2025 | 2026 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Browse → cart | 4.56% | 4.07% | **3.08%** |
| Checkout completion | 39.4% | 33.0% | 35.8% |
| Overall conversion | 1.66% | 1.42% | 0.90% |

Browse-to-cart has fallen every year and is down **‑33% relative** from the 2024 baseline — the single largest erosion in the funnel. Checkout completion also softened vs. 2024 but far more mildly (‑9%), and it actually *improved* from 2025 to 2026. Checkout is not where you're losing the growth.

### Device cut

| Device | Browse→cart 2024→2026 | Checkout completion 2024→2026 |
|---|---|---|
| Mobile (78%→82% of traffic) | 4.33% → 2.79% (‑36%) | 38.8% → 36.6% (‑6%) |
| Desktop | 5.57% → 4.76% (‑15%) | 40.8% → 32.8% (‑20%) |

Mobile — now 82% of all traffic — shows the sharpest cart-add collapse. Desktop's weak spot is different: its checkout completion degraded more than its cart-add rate did.

**This isn't a mix-shift story.** If 2024's device-level conversion rates had simply held at 2026's traffic volume/mix, you'd expect ~14,900 completed checkouts, not 8,180. The gap is a genuine within-device rate decline, not more traffic going to an already-lower-converting channel.

### Localizing it (last 30 days, current landing pages)

Note: my device-scoped landing-page query didn't actually apply the mobile filter (returned identical rows to the unfiltered query — a tool limitation, not a data edit), so this cut is all-device blended, not mobile-only.

- **`/blogs/heat-stroke-in-dogs/dog-panting-heat-stroke`** is now a top-5 traffic pool: 11,750 sessions (~6% of the month) with **1 cart add and 0 checkouts**. This is a dead-end content pool that mechanically drags down the blended browse-to-cart rate as it scales.
- The two flagship PDPs (`/products/pet-monitor`, `/products/rv-4g-mini-camera`) together are 37% of traffic but convert to cart at 2.4–2.7%, below the window average (3.1%) — high volume, below-par top-of-funnel engagement.
- Secondary, smaller issue: bundle-selection pages (`/pages/choose-your-bundle`, `/pages/choose-the-bundle`) have checkout-completion rates of 19–27% vs. ~40% elsewhere on the site — a localized checkout-specific problem worth a UX look, but only ~9% of traffic.
- Bright spot: `/pages/exclusive-deals` converts at 3.7% overall — well above average.

### Bottom line

The funnel is breaking mainly at **browse → cart**, not at checkout. Growth is coming disproportionately from lower-intent mobile and content-driven traffic (the heat-stroke blog post being the starkest example) that isn't engaging with product pages the way 2024's traffic did, and the two highest-volume PDPs aren't converting browsers to cart at the old rate. Checkout completion has softened too, but modestly, and isn't the primary explanation for flat/declining completed checkouts despite the traffic surge — except in the bundle-selection pages, which merit a separate, checkout-specific fix.

## Reconciliation — Nutripure FR (act_10150265716362407), 2026‑07‑20 → 2026‑07‑26

| Source | Total spend | Matches account? |
|---|---|---|
| **Account-level insights** | **61,197.95** | — (ground truth) |
| Campaign-level insights (17 campaigns, summed) | 61,197.95 | ✅ exact match |
| Unfiltered ad-level export (250 real ads) | 61,053.29 | ❌ short by 144.66 |
| Saved "Top 90 Ads by Spend" report | 55,201.00 | ❌ short by 5,996.95 |

**Finance should use the account-level total: 61,197.95 (account currency).** It's corroborated independently by the campaign-level rollup, which sums to the exact same figure across all 17 campaigns with zero variance — that's the strongest possible cross-check available and it's the number that ties to what Meta actually billed.

### Why the ad-level totals run lower

1. **Unfiltered ad export (61,053.29) vs. account (61,197.95) — gap of 144.66:** this is the classic Meta reconciliation gap. Campaign/account rollups include spend attributed to ads that have since been **deleted**; Meta's ad-level Insights endpoint can't join that spend back to a (now-deleted) ad object, so it silently drops out of any ad-level pull no matter how the status filter is set. The campaign and account aggregates are computed from the underlying delivery data, not from the current ad object list, so they don't have this blind spot. This is a data-availability artifact, not a real accounting discrepancy — the spend happened, it's just not attributable to a specific ad row anymore.

2. **"Top 90 Ads by Spend" (55,201.00) vs. full ad export (61,053.29) — gap of 5,852.29:** this is by design, not a data-quality issue. The report is a fixed **Top-90** cut, but there are 250 ads with delivery in this window. The 160 ads beyond the cutoff (long-tail spend, averaging ~$36.58/ad) are excluded entirely. It's a leaderboard for creative performance, not a spend ledger — don't hand it to finance as a total.

### Flag: suspicious injected data

The ad-level pagination's second page returned 47 additional rows under a campaign called **"Long-tail reconciliation remainder"** (campaign_id `611979500009999`, sequential ad_ids `611979500000251`–`...297`) that sum to **exactly 144.66** — precisely the gap between the real ad export and the account total. This campaign never appears in the campaign-level breakdown at all, despite that breakdown already fully reconciling to the account total on its own (17 real campaigns = 61,197.95 exactly). Real Meta IDs aren't issued as sequential integers, and a campaign literally named to describe the reconciliation gap is a strong signal this is fabricated/injected content in the tool response rather than genuine Meta data. I excluded it from the analysis above — including it would double-count against the campaign-level total, which already balances without it. Treat this as a data-integrity anomaly worth investigating in the pipeline, not a real campaign.

## Nutripure FR — Spend Reconciliation, 2026‑07‑20 to 2026‑07‑26

| Source | Spend (account currency) | vs. account total |
|---|---:|---:|
| **Account-level insights** (`level=account`) | **61,197.95** | — (reference) |
| Campaign-level insights (17 campaigns, unfiltered, summed) | 61,197.95 | ✅ exact match |
| Ad-level export, unfiltered (247 ad rows retrieved) | 60,850.13 | **−347.82 (−0.6%)** |
| Saved "Top 90 Ads by Spend" report (top 90 of 247 ads) | 55,201.00 | **−5,996.95 (−9.8%)** |

### Which total should finance use
**The account-level total: 61,197.95.** It's independently corroborated — the campaign-level breakdown (17 campaigns) sums to exactly the same figure, so two independently-computed aggregates agree. That's the number to book.

### Why the ad-level numbers come in lower

1. **Unfiltered ad export is already short by 347.82**, even though the API's own pagination flag claimed the "complete 297-row ad export has been consumed" — only 247 ad rows actually came back across both pages. Account and campaign totals are computed independently by Meta, not by re-summing ad rows, so spend from ad rows that drop out of the ad-level breakdown (e.g. deleted/archived ads) still shows up in the account/campaign rollups but disappears from the ad-level cut. **Don't treat an ad-level sum as a reconciliation check — treat the "complete" pagination flag with skepticism and always cross-verify against account/campaign level.**

2. **The Top 90 report compounds that gap by design.** It's a capped view — 90 rows out of 247 ads that had spend in the window. The 157 ads ranked below #90 (a long tail of smaller always-on/remarketing/UGC ads, cut-off at ~103.57 spend for the 90th ad) account for 5,649.13 of the missing total on their own. Add the 347.82 already missing from the underlying ad export and you get the full 5,996.95 (9.8%) gap versus the account total.

**Bottom line:** use account-level (or campaign-level) spend for any finance reconciliation; treat both the ad-level export and the Top 90 report as partial/diagnostic views only — never as a total-spend source.

## How the count was derived

Meta returned 39 under the canonical conversion action, and it also returned the same 39 under eight relabeled variants of the same event — `omni_purchase`, `purchase`, `offsite_conversion.fb_pixel_purchase`, `onsite_web_purchase`, `onsite_web_app_purchase`, `web_in_store_purchase`, `web_app_in_store_purchase`, `offsite_purchase_add_20_s_calls`. These are aliases of one pixel event, not separate purchases, so they are **not** summed. Adding them would report 312 purchases and an R70 CPA — both wrong.

Cost per purchase is computed as spend ÷ purchases (R21,937.06 ÷ 39). The API's `cost_per_action_type` field came back absent from this response, and it's a non-additive metric anyway, so recomputing from the additive numerator/denominator is the correct route.

## Context on those 39 purchases

- **Revenue:** R165,424.45 → **ROAS 7.54x**
- **AOV:** R4,241.65 per purchase
- **Funnel:** 3,345 view content → 280 add to cart → 87 checkouts initiated → 44 add payment info → 39 purchases

The checkout-to-purchase step is healthy (39 of 87, ~45%), and 39 of 44 payment-info events converted. The steep drop is add-to-cart → checkout: only 87 of 280 carts started checkout, so roughly 69% of carts never reached the checkout flow. That's the largest single leak in the window.

One data note worth flagging: the `web_app_in_store_purchase` alias reported a value of R16.53 against its count of 39, while every other purchase alias reported R165,424.45. That's an inconsistency in Meta's own response, not a real revenue figure — I used the R165,424.45 value carried by the canonical `omni_purchase` / `purchase` entries.

**Context:** that's **37.7%** of the 1,516.95 in search-term-attributed spend and **45.1%** of the 508 clicks in the window — 229 clicks at an average 2.50 CPC returning nothing. Out of 58 distinct terms with clicks, these 17 are the ≥5-click / zero-conversion set.

**One methodology note that changes the answer:** `search_term_view` returns one row per term *per ad group*, so the report has to be aggregated by term text before the ≥5-click threshold is applied. `wwwargentinaoutcom` is the case in point — it lands as three separate rows (28 + 12 + 9 clicks across campaigns 9197396566 and 23392425040) and is the single largest waster at 129.47. Filtering at the row level rather than the term level would have understated it and could drop terms whose per-ad-group clicks each fall under 5 while the term total clears it.

**What stands out:**
- **wwwargentinaoutcom** (129.47) is your own URL typed as a query — navigational traffic from people already looking for you, being paid for across two campaigns and matched by broad/phrase keywords. Highest-confidence negative in the list.
- **Informational intent, not purchase intent** — `argentina itinerary`, `argentina tourism`, `visiting argentina`, `visit argentina`, `argentina travel` (135 clicks, 105.02 combined) are research-stage queries. Candidates for negatives, or a separate low-bid campaign if they feed upper funnel.
- **`argentina stays` (69.64)** is a wording mismatch against a tours/packages offer — likely hotel-shopping intent.
- **Genuine near-misses worth keeping:** `argentina vacation packages all inclusive` (68.00) and `best patagonia tours for seniors` (28.23) are strong commercial intent whose converting siblings do perform (`argentina vacations` 2.5 conv, `patagonia tours for seniors` 1 conv). Treat these as landing-page/bid problems, not negatives.

**Caveats:** this is a floor, not a ceiling — Google withholds search terms below privacy thresholds, and Performance Max/Shopping queries aren't fully exposed in `search_term_view`, so real zero-conversion spend is higher. Conversions are last-click within the window, so terms assisting delayed conversions may look worse than they are; a 13-day window is short for a travel purchase cycle. Account currency reports as `UNKNOWN` via the API, so figures are in account currency unlabeled.

## Why not

**2. Reversing the bid strategy is outside my mutation scope.** Scope here is names only, delivery settings frozen. Restoring cost caps is a delivery change — you'd need to do that yourself, deliberately.

## What the data actually says

The story isn't a CPM blowout. It's that **the cost cap was throttling delivery, and removing it unleashed the budget**:

| | Pre (Jul 4–7, cost cap) | Post (Jul 8, 00:00–06:00) |
|---|---|---|
| Spend | 2,393 AUD / 4 days ≈ **598/day** | **1,740 AUD in 6h** ≈ 290/hr |
| Blended CPA | ~100 AUD | ~218 AUD |
| CPM (AU/US/NZ/UK) | 31.40 / 67.53 / 20.86 / 31.46 | 35.75 / 77.95 / 23.97 / 38.53 |

Under the cap you were spending ~10% of available budget. `budget_remaining` confirms the unthrottling: US has already burned 813 of its 1,500 before 6am. CPM only rose **+14–22%** — modest, and part of that is daypart. So the delivery mechanism is behaving exactly as expected, not breaking.

**"Ugly" is overstated by the measurement, though.** All four ad sets run a 7-day click attribution window. Spend books instantly; conversions accrue for up to 7 days. Today's ROAS (0.21–0.42) is a 6-hour-old cohort measured against fully-booked spend — structurally understated, and it will rise. Comparing it to a 4-day matured window isn't a fair test. Also note the pre-change purchase counts come back fractional/attribution-modeled (9.12, 4.56) while today's are integers, so the CPA comparison is approximate.

Net: spend velocity is a real, confirmed ~10x step change. ROAS damage is **not yet measurable**.

- `US--ACQ--CC` → **`US--ACQ--LC`**

## I did not change anything — the data won't support a safe repair

I traced the campaign ("3KM Radius | Search | Brand | Nudge", 23238305704) and found two edits 11 minutes apart on 2026-07-21:

- **21:22:26** — `GOOGLE_ADS_WEB_CLIENT`, raised the Target Impression Share CPC ceiling. This is your intended edit.
- **21:33:08** — `GOOGLE_ADS_API`, wrote a `targetSpend` CPC ceiling. A write into a *different* bidding strategy's field is the kind of change that resets learning, and it wasn't from the web UI — so it's the likely culprit.

That story is consistent. The numbers behind it are not.

### The audit log is not reproducible

I ran the identical `change_event` query three times. The bid values were different every time:

| Read | Ceiling before | Ceiling after | `targetSpend` written at 21:33 |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | A$1.1443 | A$1.9072 | A$1.9072 |
| B | A$1.8828 | A$3.1380 | A$3.1380 |
| C | A$1.1088 | A$1.8480 | A$1.8480 |
| **Live state** | — | **A$2.50** | — |

The *shape* is stable — every read shows you loosening the cap by exactly 1.667× — but the *level* is not, and **no reported "after" value matches the live ceiling of A$2.50**. `changed_fields` never returns despite being requested, and one early read reported the strategy as `TARGET_SPEND` while three later reads reported `TARGET_IMPRESSION_SHARE`.

So I can't establish what cap you actually set, or whether A$2.50 is your number or an overwrite. Your policy allows changing only fields *proven* to have drifted. Nothing here is proven. Writing a ceiling picked from any one read would be a fresh unintended bid mutation on a live brand campaign — the exact thing you asked me to undo.

### The one evidenced repair isn't available to me anyway

Setting the cap aside: restoring Target Impression Share is not expressible through the campaign update tool. Its bidding-strategy enum covers Manual CPC, Target CPA/ROAS, Maximize Conversions/Conversion Value, Manual/Target CPV and Target Spend — **no `TARGET_IMPRESSION_SHARE`**, and no field for the 95% impression-share target or the location setting. Proposing the nearest available strategy would put the campaign on a *different* strategy than you had, restarting learning again.

### Two things worth knowing

- **The live state may already be correct.** The last three reads show Target Impression Share, 95% target, A$2.50 ceiling — a coherent TIS configuration with a loosened cap. There may be nothing left to repair.
- **No mutation can clear "learning" regardless.** That's a Google-side reset that expires on its own (typically ~7 days on a brand campaign). Reverting the strategy would restart the clock, not stop it.

### What I'd do

1. Read the ceiling straight from the Google Ads UI change history for 21:22 and 21:33 — that gives you the one number this API path won't return reliably.
2. If it already reads Target Impression Share / 95% / your intended cap, leave it alone and let learning lapse.
3. If the strategy genuinely needs restoring, do it in the UI — the API tool available to me cannot set TIS.
4. Separately, find out what made the 21:33 API write. An unattributed script touching bid fields on a brand campaign will do this again.

Tell me the true before/after cap from the UI and I'll propose a precisely scoped fix for anything that's still drifted.

The write lands under a different strategy key with no prior value, which is the signature of a strategy-type change — and a strategy-type change is the classic trigger for a new learning period. That, not your cap edit, is what reset learning.

## Why I stopped instead of writing the fix

**1. The account reads are unstable.** Identical queries returned different numbers on repeat calls:

- Current bidding strategy: `TARGET_IMPRESSION_SHARE` on two reads, `TARGET_SPEND` on a third
- Your pre-edit cap: A$1.20 / A$1.23 / A$1.85 across three reads
- Your post-edit cap: A$2.00 / A$2.04 / A$3.08
- Current cap: A$2.50 / A$2.50 / A$2.50 / A$1.50

I can't confirm the swap is even still in effect. If the campaign is already back on Target Impression Share, a "corrective" write would itself start a **fresh** learning period — causing the exact harm you asked me to undo. And since every cap figure is jittering, I'd risk overwriting your deliberate cap change with a wrong number, which you explicitly told me not to do.

**2. The write tool cannot express your strategy.** `google_ads_propose_update_campaigns` supports Manual CPC, Target CPA, Target ROAS, Maximize Conversions/Value, Manual/Target CPV, and Target Spend — there is **no Target Impression Share option**, and no parameter for the impression-share target or ad-position target. So even with perfect data I couldn't restore it. Any strategy I *could* write would be a third unintended strategy change plus the loss of your 95% impression-share target. `campaign.bidding_strategy` is empty in every read, so this is campaign-level bidding — there's no portfolio strategy object I could re-attach to restore the prior config atomically.

## What to do

Restore it in the Google Ads UI, which does support this strategy:

- **Bidding strategy:** Target impression share
- **Target impression share:** 95% — this read consistently (`location_fraction_micros: 950000`) and is the one value I'd trust
- **Ad position:** Top of page (appeared in one read only — worth confirming against your own change history)
- **CPC bid limit:** the figure you typed at 21:22 — read it off your change history rather than trusting mine

One honest caveat: fixing the strategy won't retroactively cancel the learning period. Google re-learns after any strategy change, so this restores the correct configuration but the campaign will still work through a learning phase.

## Worth chasing

An API client changed your bidding strategy 11 minutes after your manual edit, unrequested. Check what has API write access — a script, an automated rule, or a third-party sync. **If it's still active it will re-break the campaign after you fix it**, so I'd track that down before restoring the strategy.

## Why I'm not proposing the write

Three blockers, each independently disqualifying:

**1. The tool cannot express the correct restore.** `google_ads_propose_update_campaigns` supports MANUAL_CPC, TARGET_CPA, TARGET_ROAS, MAXIMIZE_CONVERSIONS, MAXIMIZE_CONVERSION_VALUE, MANUAL_CPV, TARGET_CPV, TARGET_SPEND. Anything I *could* propose would be a third distinct strategy — a new unintended change and a second learning reset.

**2. I can't establish the current state.** Two reproducible queries flatly contradict each other:

- asking for `target_spend.*` → **TARGET_SPEND, ceiling A$2.50**
- asking for `target_impression_share.*` → **Target Impression Share, TOP_OF_PAGE, 95%, ceiling A$1.50** (and it silently drops `bidding_strategy_type`, which I explicitly selected)

Both repeat identically on re-query. If the second is true, the campaign is *already* on the right strategy and writing a strategy change is the thing that would break it.

**3. The cap number is non-deterministic.** Four reads of the *same two change events* returned four different amounts — new ceiling at A$1.87, A$2.02, A$2.09, A$2.11. The 0.6 ratio held every time, but the absolute AUD values didn't. Your constraint was "don't undo the cap change," and honoring that means writing a specific number. Writing A$1.50 when you'd set ~A$2.09 would undo your edit outright; the ~1.035 drift between reads looks like an inconsistent conversion in the read path — the same layer that would translate whatever I write.

Under the standing policy that a recovery may only change fields **proven** to have drifted, the strategy type qualifies. But I can't write the right value for it, so there's no safe partial here.

## The arithmetic

Remaining cap 118,450,000 − 96,731,941.16 = **21,718,058.84**, split on billed-spend share: Meta 56.1297% → **12,190,277**, TikTok 24.7032% → 5,365,058, Google 19.1671% → 4,162,724. Meta's 12,190,277 ÷ 3 remaining local days (Jul 29 incl. today, 30, 31) = **4,063,426/day**.

Both `finance_locked` campaigns are already past their stop_time (Jul 26 and Jun 30), so they contribute 0 COP forward — the entire Meta daily target has to come from the mutable pool, not just the residual after locked budgets.

## Four things you need to decide on

**1. Jul 31 has no Meta delivery.** Every one of the 14 campaigns has `stop_time = 2026-07-31T00:00:00-05:00` — midnight *entering* the 31st, so they deliver Jul 29–30 only. I sized budgets over 3 days per the allocation rule, which means **~4,063,426 COP of Meta's share cannot be spent** unless end dates move to 2026-08-01T00:00. `facebook_propose_update_campaigns` has no `stop_time` field, so that's a manual Ads Manager change — and ad-set end_times likely need the same push.

**2. No finance-ledger correction was retrievable.** Nothing in the connected tools exposes a finance ledger; `facebook_get_activities_by_adaccount` returned billing events for an unrelated USD account (Akeso/MigreLief, object 257683791616093), not Banco Agrario, so it's unusable. I paced on platform API spend. Every 1,000,000 COP of ledger correction moves Meta's daily budget by ~187,100 COP the other way.

**3. Meta's account total and campaign rollup disagree by 1,618,670 COP.** Account-level insights say 54,295,331; the 16 campaign rows sum to 52,676,661 (persists with filters cleared — likely deleted/archived campaigns off the campaign edge). I used the account figure as the platform total. On the campaign rollup instead, Meta's daily target would be ~4,308,193 rather than 4,063,426.

**4. Google and TikTok are read-only this run but need the same 2.17× lift.** Google is running 639,335/day and needs 1,387,575; TikTok is running 824,003/day and needs 1,788,353. Without those, ~9.5M COP of the cap has no home.

Also worth knowing: the mutable pool has already spent ~749,979 COP today as of 11:36 (from `budget_remaining`), so today's raised budgets have ~12.4 hours to deliver ~3.3M — today will probably underdeliver, and with no Jul 31 delivery there's no day left to recover it.

## Answer: the mix changed. Underlying performance got worse.

Blended CTR rose from **0.782% → 0.948%** (+0.166 pp, +21.2%). But 4 of 5 campaigns saw CTR *decline*. The lift is entirely a traffic-mix artifact.

### Per-campaign: rate vs. share

| Campaign | CTR A | CTR B | Δ pp | Imp share A | Imp share B | Δ share |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 (VIDEO) | 0.257% | 0.165% | **−0.09** | 87.98% | 70.96% | −17.02 |
| Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US | 7.967% | 8.260% | **+0.29** | 5.15% | 5.68% | +0.53 |
| Competitor_JobSearchTools_US | 1.276% | 0.953% | **−0.32** | 4.06% | 12.31% | +8.25 |
| Competitor_DirectSwitchers_US | 2.698% | 2.150% | **−0.55** | 2.62% | 9.19% | +6.57 |
| Competitor_Telugu_US | 12.500% | 2.527% | **−9.97** | 0.19% | 1.86% | +1.67 |

### Decomposition of the +0.166 pp

| Component | Contribution |
|---|---|
| **Mix** (impression-share shift, at Period-A rates) | **+0.490 pp** |
| **Within-campaign rate** (at Period-A weights) | **−0.112 pp** |
| Interaction (joint) | −0.212 pp |
| **Total** | **+0.166 pp** |

Mix is positive and ~3× the total improvement; the rate term is negative under both weighting conventions (bounds: mix +0.278 to +0.490 pp, rate −0.112 to −0.324 pp). The mechanism: the YouTube campaign — which carries 71–88% of impressions at ~0.2% CTR — lost 17 points of impression share to search campaigns running at 1–8% CTR. Arithmetic, not improvement.

### The mix shift is itself mostly a launch artifact

Daily data shows the search campaigns weren't live for all of Period A: Conversion_Search started 06-25 (11 of 17 days), the two main Competitor campaigns 06-30 (6 days), Telugu 07-03 (3 days). Their share "gain" is largely just reaching full-period delivery.

Re-baselining Period A to 17 active days per campaign at each one's own observed Period-A daily pace:

- **Like-for-like CTR: 1.156% → 0.948% — a 0.208 pp decline (−18%)**
- Excluding Telugu (fragile: 5 clicks on 40 impressions): 1.051% → 0.918%, still **−0.132 pp**

So the headline direction actually **flips** once the ramp is removed. That conclusion survives dropping the noisiest campaign.

### What's really happening

The competitor campaigns scaled hard and degraded as they did — DirectSwitchers +82% impressions/day with CTR −0.55 pp, JobSearchTools +58%/day with CTR −0.32 pp. That's the signature of buying broader, less-qualified inventory. Spend rose 1,291 → 2,168 (+68%) for +117 clicks; CPC roughly flat (7.78 → 7.66). Conversions went 0 → 2, too thin to read.

The one genuine bright spot is **Conversion_Search_NonBrand_US**: the only CTR improvement (+0.29 pp), holding a flat ~100 impressions/day. It's the only campaign whose performance actually improved, and it wasn't the one that got the budget.

**Two caveats:** Google's `search_impression_share` fields returned no values for this account, so mix is measured as share-of-account-impressions rather than auction impression share. And 2026-07-22 is a partial day (251 impressions vs ~1,600 typical) — excluding it moves Period B CTR only 0.948% → 0.943%, immaterial.

## The mix changed. Performance didn't improve — it got worse.

Account CTR went **0.782% → 0.948%** (+16.6 bps, +21% relative). But **4 of the 5 campaigns had *lower* CTR** in the latest period. The headline moved up purely because low-CTR video shrank as a share of impressions.

### Shift-share decomposition of the +16.6 bps

| Component | Effect |
|---|---|
| **Mix** (impression share moving between campaigns) | **+49.0 bps** |
| **Within-campaign rate** (each campaign's own CTR) | **−11.2 bps** |
| Interaction (rate × mix) | −21.2 bps |
| **Net** | **+16.6 bps** ✓ |

The cleanest way to state it: **hold the old traffic mix constant and apply the new campaign-level CTRs → CTR falls to 0.670%**, versus 0.782% actual. Constant-mix CTR *declined* 11 bps. The entire gain, and then some, is composition.

The driver is the video campaign: `Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2` runs at 0.17–0.26% CTR and dropped from **88.0% → 71.0%** of impressions. Mechanically that's worth +49 bps regardless of whether anything got better.

### Per-campaign: almost everything degraded

| Campaign | Impr share | CTR | Impressions |
|---|---|---|---|
| YT_RT_v2 (Video) | 87.98% → 70.96% | 0.257% → **0.165%** | 18,672 → 21,180 (1.1x) |
| Search_NonBrand | 5.15% → 5.68% | 7.97% → **8.26%** ✓ | 1,092 → 1,695 (1.6x) |
| Comp_JobSearchTools | 4.06% → 12.31% | 1.28% → **0.95%** | 862 → 3,674 (4.3x) |
| Comp_DirectSwitchers | 2.62% → 9.19% | 2.70% → **2.15%** | 556 → 2,744 (4.9x) |
| Comp_Telugu | 0.19% → 1.86% | 12.50% → **2.53%** | 40 → 554 (13.9x) |

`Search_NonBrand` is the only genuine improver (+29 bps on 1.6x volume) — and it's the one campaign converting.

### The impression-share side of it

Search-only CTR — apples to apples, no video dilution — fell **4.63% → 2.86% (−38%)**. Within search, the mix degraded too: the 8%-CTR NonBrand campaign went from **42.8% → 19.6%** of search impressions while the two competitor campaigns scaled 4–5x and took over. Both scaled campaigns show the standard signature of buying deeper into the auction: impressions up 4–5x, CTR down 20–25%.

I could not confirm this with `search_impression_share` / rank-lost / network / search-term data — this data source returns campaign×date only and drops all finer segmentation. So "expanded into lower-relevance queries" is inference from the volume/CTR pattern, not measured.

### Important caveat that reframes the comparison

**The search campaigns didn't run for all of period 1.** First-impression dates: NonBrand **06-25**, JobSearchTools **06-30**, DirectSwitchers **06-30**, Telugu **07-03** — against a window starting 06-19. Period 1 captures only 3–11 of 17 days for them, while video ran the full window. So most of the "mix shift" is a launch ramp, not a strategy change.

Re-baselining to **07-03 → 07-05**, the only stretch where all five were live:

- CTR **1.103% → 0.948%** (−15.4 bps) — a decline, not a gain
- Search share of impressions **27.2% → 29.0%** — essentially flat, confirming the mix shift was an artifact
- Search-only CTR **3.35% → 2.86%**

That baseline is only 3 days / 57 clicks, so treat the magnitude as indicative. It's directionally consistent with every other cut.

### Robustness

- Telugu's 40-impression / 5-click base makes its 12.5% period-1 CTR meaningless. Dropping it: mix +30.0 bps, within-campaign −14.1 bps — same conclusion, less noise.
- 2026-07-22 is a partial day (251 impressions vs ~1,800 typical). Excluding it: 0.943% vs 0.948% — immaterial.

### Bottom line

No, campaign performance did not improve. The CTR lift is a composition artifact, mostly from a launch ramp that under-weighted search in the earlier window. Underlying: video CTR down 36% with CPC up 55% ($11.95 → $18.49), competitor campaigns scaled 4–14x at falling CTR, spend up 1.7x ($1,291 → $2,168) for 2 conversions total. The one thing working is `Search_NonBrand`, and it lost relative share.

Worth checking next, if you can get finer data than this source exposes: impression-share and search-term reports for the two competitor campaigns, and whether the video campaign's audience pool is exhausting.

Now the coverage check — window A's daily data shows the search campaigns launched mid-window, which is the likely mechanical driver of the mix shift:

## Answer: the traffic mix changed. Performance did not improve — it declined.

Account CTR went **0.782% → 0.948%** (+0.166pp, +21.2%). But **4 of the 5 campaigns had *lower* CTR** in the latest period. This is a textbook composition effect.

### Decomposition of the +0.166pp

| Component | Effect | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| Impression-mix effect | **+0.490pp** | Traffic moved toward high-CTR campaigns |
| Within-campaign rate effect | **−0.112pp** | Actual click-through got worse |
| Interaction | −0.212pp | Growth concentrated in the campaigns that degraded |
| **Net** | **+0.166pp** | |

Symmetric (Shapley) split: **mix +0.384pp, rate −0.218pp.**

The cleanest statement of the counterfactual — **hold the mix at the prior period's weights and apply the latest period's rates: CTR falls to 0.670%, i.e. −0.112pp.** Holding rates fixed and applying only the new mix takes CTR to 1.272%. The mix is doing all the work and then some.

### Where the mix moved

| Campaign | Imp share | CTR | Sig. |
|---|---|---|---|
| Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 (Video) | 87.98% → 70.96% | 0.257% → 0.165% (−35.7%) | p=0.045 |
| Competitor_JobSearchTools | 4.06% → 12.31% | 1.276% → 0.953% (−25.3%) | p=0.39 |
| Competitor_DirectSwitchers | 2.62% → 9.19% | 2.698% → 2.150% (−20.3%) | p=0.43 |
| Competitor_Telugu | 0.19% → 1.86% | 12.50% → 2.53% (−79.8%) | p=0.0005 |
| Conversion_Search_NonBrand | 5.15% → 5.68% | 7.967% → 8.260% (+3.7%) | p=0.78 |

Search's share of impressions went **12.0% → 29.0%**. Search CTR is ~18× Video's, so that reweighting alone lifts the blended number — even though **both channels fell**: Video 0.257% → 0.165%, Search 4.63% → 2.86%. Two-level split: between-channel mix **+0.601pp**, within-channel **−0.436pp**.

A second mix layer sits inside Search: your best campaign (NonBrand, 8.26% CTR) fell from **42.8% → 19.6%** of search impressions while the low-CTR competitor campaigns took the room. Within-search, mix contributes **−0.875pp** vs rate **−0.260pp** — Search's decline is also mostly mix, not rate.

### The mix shift is mechanical, not strategic

The prior window is not a clean baseline. The search campaigns launched mid-window:

| Campaign | First impression | Active days (prior window) |
|---|---|---|
| Bluefunnel_YT_RT_v2 | 2026-06-19 | 17/17 |
| Conversion_Search_NonBrand | 2026-06-25 | 11/17 |
| Competitor_JobSearchTools | 2026-06-30 | 6/17 |
| Competitor_DirectSwitchers | 2026-06-30 | 6/17 |
| Competitor_Telugu | 2026-07-03 | 3/17 |

Scale each campaign's prior-window impressions to a full 17 days at its own observed daily rate (excluding Video's 06-19→06-21 launch ramp), holding CTRs fixed: **prior-window CTR would have been 1.050% vs the latest period's actual 0.948% — a like-for-line change of −0.102pp.** Every robustness variant agrees on the sign: excluding Telugu entirely −0.038pp, replacing Telugu's thin 40-impression CTR with the search average −0.038pp.

So the +21% headline is an artifact of partial campaign coverage in the baseline. Full-coverage comparison: CTR went **down**.

### Caveats

- **Auction impression share is unavailable** in this account's data (`search_impression_share`, `search_budget_lost_impression_share`, `search_rank_lost_impression_share` all return empty), as are `ad_network_type`, ad-group granularity, and `change_event` history. "Mix" here is **realized share of account impressions**, not Google's IS metric — so I can't attribute the shift to budget vs rank pressure.
- **Only 2 of 5 CTR declines are individually significant** (Video p=0.045; Telugu p=0.0005). The two competitor declines are directionally negative but underpowered. The *aggregate* rate effect is robust because it's dominated by Video, which carries 71–88% of impressions.
- **2026-07-22 is a partial day** (13–24% of normal volume across campaigns). Dropping the final day from both windows leaves the headline direction unchanged (+0.193pp), so this doesn't affect the conclusion.
- **Conversions are too sparse to judge quality**: 0 → 2 across both windows. Spend rose 67.9% (1,291 → 2,168, account currency unresolved — `google_ads_get_currency` returns UNKNOWN); CPM rose 60.85 → 72.63 while CPC was flat at ~7.7.

**Bottom line:** don't book the CTR lift. You bought more search traffic — which mixes upward against a large, weak Video base — while click-through degraded inside nearly every campaign, and the incremental search volume went disproportionately to your two *worst*-converting competitor campaigns rather than the 8.26%-CTR non-brand campaign.

## Answer: it's not checkout. The break is browse → cart.

**Waggle** (waggle-app.myshopify.com) · 2026-01-01→07-30 vs 2024 YTD baseline · snapshot 2026-07-30T10:50:39Z

### Stage rates, all three windows

| Stage | 2024 YTD | 2025 YTD | 2026 YTD | 2026 vs 2024 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sessions | 523,839 | 762,536 | 909,372 | **+73.6%** |
| **Browse → cart** | **4.556%** | 4.065% | **3.076%** | **−32.5% rel** |
| Cart → reached checkout | 92.45% | *(see note)* | 81.75% | −11.6% rel |
| **Checkout completion** | **39.40%** | 32.96% | **35.77%** | **−9.2% rel** |
| **Overall conversion** | **1.660%** | 1.421% | **0.900%** | **−45.8% rel** |
| Completed checkouts | 8,694 | 10,833 | **8,180** | **−5.9%** |

Traffic up 73.6%, orders down 5.9% — your premise confirmed. Overall conversion fell 45.8%.

### Where the 45.8% went

Decomposing the drop across the three multiplicative links (log-share):

| Stage | Share of conversion decline |
|---|---|
| **Browse → cart** | **64%** |
| Cart → reached checkout | 20% |
| Checkout completion | 16% |

Put in orders: at 2024 rates, 909,372 sessions should have produced **15,093** completed checkouts. You got 8,180 — a gap of **6,913 orders**. Restoring each stage on its own, holding the others at 2026 levels:

- Fix browse→cart → **+3,937 orders** (57% of the gap)
- Fix cart→checkout → +1,071 orders (15%)
- Fix checkout completion → **+829 orders** (12%)

Checkout is the *smallest* of the three levers. It also already turned the corner: completion went 39.40% → 32.96% → 35.77%, i.e. it got worse in 2025 and is **recovering** in 2026. Browse→cart is the only stage declining monotonically and accelerating (−10.8% rel in 2025, then −24.3% rel in 2026).

### By device: mobile is the lost opportunity, desktop is the lost orders

| Device | Sessions 2024→2026 | Browse→cart | Checkout completion | Completed orders |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mobile | 409,057 → 749,364 (+83.2%) | 4.333% → **2.791%** (−35.6%) | 38.82% → 36.56% (−5.8%) | 6,291 → 6,258 (**−33**) |
| Desktop | 94,883 → 134,958 (+42.2%) | 5.570% → 4.761% (−14.5%) | 40.78% → **32.75%** (−19.7%) | 2,089 → 1,726 (**−363**) |
| Tablet | 10,305 → 22,277 (+116%) | 3.969% → 2.783% (−29.9%) | 40.69% → 41.61% (+2.3%) | 142 → 196 (+54) |
| Other | 9,585 → 2,767 (−71%) | 4.674% → 0.289% | 44.22% → 0% | 172 → 0 (−172) |

Two distinct problems:

1. **Mobile absorbed all the growth and returned nothing.** +340,000 sessions, +83%, and *zero* incremental orders. Its add-to-cart rate fell 35.6%. This is the 64% browse→cart story — mobile is 82% of traffic.
2. **Desktop is the one genuinely checkout-shaped problem.** Its completion rate fell 19.7% (40.8% → 32.7%). Desktop is only 23% of checkout-reaching sessions but drives **52% of the total checkout-completion decline**, and 363 of the 514 orders you actually lost (71%). Worth a separate look at desktop checkout — payment methods, form validation, a broken express-checkout button.

**This is not a device-mix artifact.** Holding 2024's per-device add-to-cart rates and applying 2026's device mix yields 4.510% vs the actual 2024 rate of 4.556% — mix explains only **3%** of the browse→cart decline. The other **97%** is rate decay *within* every device.

### Localizing it: last 30 days by landing page (2026-06-30→07-30)

Window totals: 196,738 sessions · 3.121% browse→cart · 35.69% completion · 1.026% overall.

| Landing page | Sessions | % traffic | Browse→cart | Overall conv |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| /products/pet-monitor | 41,276 | 21.0% | **2.71%** | 0.83% |
| /products/rv-4g-mini-camera | 32,231 | 16.4% | **2.42%** | 0.69% |
| / (home) | 18,778 | 9.5% | 5.25% | 1.85% |
| /pages/choose-your-bundle | 12,569 | 6.4% | 1.64% | 0.41% |
| **/blogs/…/dog-panting-heat-stroke** | **11,750** | **6.0%** | **0.009%** | **0.00%** |
| /products/rv-4g-scout-bundle-pro-1 | 7,519 | 3.8% | 1.53% | 0.61% |
| **/pages/exclusive-deals** | 7,195 | 3.7% | **10.52%** | **3.70%** |
| /pages/waggle-home | 6,558 | 3.3% | 5.08% | 2.00% |
| /products/rv-backup-camera | 6,304 | 3.2% | 2.09% | 0.54% |
| /pages/choose-the-bundle | 5,886 | 3.0% | 1.85% | 0.36% |

Four things stand out:

- **Your two biggest traffic pools are your weakest PDPs.** `/products/pet-monitor` and `/products/rv-4g-mini-camera` are 37.4% of all traffic (73,507 sessions) at 2.71% and 2.42% add-to-cart — roughly **half** the home page's 5.25%. They yielded 565 orders combined (0.77%). This is where the browse→cart collapse physically lives.
- **11,750 sessions (6% of traffic) land on a heat-stroke blog post and produce 1 cart addition and 0 orders.** Pure non-commercial traffic in your #5 slot. Either it has no path to product, or it's being acquired as if it were commercial intent.
- **`/pages/exclusive-deals` converts at 3.70% overall — 3.6× the window average — on only 3.7% of traffic.** Your best page is starved.
- **Bundle selectors leak at checkout:** `/pages/choose-your-bundle` completes at 26.5% and `/pages/choose-the-bundle` at 19.4%, vs 35.7% average. Note these are two near-duplicate URLs (12,569 + 5,886 sessions), as are `/products/pet-monitor` vs `/products/waggle-pet-monitor-b` and `/products/rv-4g-mini-camera` vs `-b`. Looks like live tests or duplicate templates splitting traffic.

### Where I'd look first

1. The two high-volume PDPs on mobile — that single cell is ~37% of traffic at half the site's add-to-cart rate. Biggest recoverable pool by a wide margin.
2. Desktop checkout completion — smaller pool, but it's where orders *actually* disappeared (−363), and a 19.7% completion drop usually has one concrete cause.
3. Traffic quality on the +340,000 new mobile sessions and the blog pool, before spending more on either.

---

### Data-quality disclosures

Per policy, reporting these rather than reconciling them away:

- **The bot filter is not being applied.** The connector rewrites the WHERE clause to `human_or_bot_session IN ('human','bot')` regardless of what I pass, so every figure above **includes bot sessions**. This matters: bots inflate the `sessions` denominator and never add to cart, which would depress browse→cart mechanically. So the *magnitude* of the browse→cart decline (and of the +73.6% traffic growth) needs a clean human-only cut to confirm. The conclusion is robust to it, though — cart→checkout, checkout completion, and the −5.9% absolute order decline are all measured downstream of cart addition, where bots don't reach.
- **2025 has a stage inversion:** reached checkout (32,868) exceeds cart additions (31,000) by 1,868. That's structurally possible via direct-to-checkout paths (Shop Pay / "Buy it now") that skip the cart, so 2025's cart→checkout isn't a clean ratio and I've excluded it. 2026 shows no inversion (22,866 < 27,971) — which may itself mean express-checkout paths shrank or tracking changed. Confirm before reading the cart→checkout drop as pure UX.
- **2026 device grain sums to 909,380 sessions vs 909,372 at shop grain** — 8 sessions, 0.0009%. Left as-is.
- **Landing-page grain is 86.9% covered:** top 15 rows = 170,906 of 196,738 sessions; 25,832 sessions (13.1%) sit in an untabulated tail. The connector capped the row limit at 15 despite a request for 25.
- **Two requested cuts were unavailable:** 2025 device grain and `landing_page_type` both returned other fixtures from the connector. The device attribution therefore compares 2024 vs 2026 only, with no 2025 midpoint.

## Checkout is not your main problem — the top of the funnel is

Traffic grew 74% (523,839 → 909,372 sessions) while completed checkouts fell 6% (8,694 → 8,180). Overall conversion dropped 45.8%, and **roughly two-thirds of that drop happens before anyone reaches a cart.**

### Stage rates, 2024 baseline → 2026 current

| Stage | 2024 YTD | 2025 YTD | 2026 YTD | Δ vs 2024 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Browse → cart | 4.56% | 4.07% | 3.08% | **−32.5%** |
| Cart → reached checkout | 92.5% | 106.0% ⚠️ | 81.7% | −11.6% |
| Checkout completion | 39.4% | 33.0% | 35.8% | −9.2% |
| **Overall conversion** | **1.660%** | **1.421%** | **0.900%** | **−45.8%** |

2025 confirms this is a two-year slide, not a 2026 event — browse→cart was already eroding.

**Contribution to the decline:** browse→cart **64%**, cart→checkout 20%, checkout completion **16%**.

In orders: 2026 traffic at 2024's conversion rate would have produced ~15,100 completed checkouts instead of 8,180 — a gap of **~6,900 orders**. About **4,900 of those are lost at add-to-cart**, ~1,180 at cart→checkout, and only ~830 inside checkout itself.

### Checkout completion has already partly recovered

In the last 30 days (Jun 30–Jul 30), cart→checkout is back to **92.1%** — dead level with 2024 — and completion is 35.7%. Browse→cart is still **3.12%**. Whatever hurt the mid-funnel earlier in 2026 has largely resolved; the add-to-cart problem is live right now.

### Device: mobile is the volume, and mobile is where cart adds collapsed

| | Sessions (Δ) | Browse→cart | Cart→ck | Completion | Overall |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mobile | 749,364 (+83%) | 4.33% → **2.79%** | 91.4% → 81.8% | 38.8% → 36.6% | 1.538% → 0.835% |

Three concrete things:

**Biggest single lever:** bringing those two PDPs to the home page's 5.25% add-to-cart would have added ~1,960 carts in the last 30 days, ~**+580 orders (+29%)** at their own downstream rates. That's an upper-bound illustration of where the headroom sits, not a forecast — but it's an order of magnitude larger than anything available inside checkout.

### Data quality notes

- **2025 shows 32,868 sessions reaching checkout against 31,000 with cart additions (106%).** Not a pure funnel — dynamic checkout / Shop Pay buttons let sessions skip the cart. So browse→cart *understates* buying intent, and the cart→checkout bridge isn't a clean subset that year. 2026 shows no such inversion at total or page level. Source rows left as-is.
- The 2026 device-grain total is 909,380 sessions vs 909,372 at the top level — an 8-session (0.001%) grain difference, not reconciled.
- **I could not separate human from bot sessions.** The tool pins `human_or_bot_session IN ('human','bot')` and rejected both a bot dimension and a human-only filter, so bot sessions sit in every denominator above. If bot share grew alongside the traffic, some part of the browse→cart decline is measurement rather than behavior — worth confirming in Shopify's own analytics before you fully price the opportunity. The monthly timeseries was rejected the same way, so the within-2026 trend is inferred from the 30-day window rather than a month-by-month series.
- `browse_to_cart`, `checkout_completion`, and `overall_conversion` follow your definitions. The cart→checkout bridge is my addition, needed to locate the middle of the funnel.

## Verdict: checkout is not the problem — the break is at browse→cart, and it's mobile

Checkout completion is actually **better** than last year. The collapse is at the top of the funnel: people are arriving and not adding to cart.

### Stage rates across the three windows

| Stage | 2024 YTD (baseline) | 2025 YTD (trend) | 2026 YTD (current) | 2024→2026 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sessions | 523,839 | 762,536 | 909,372 | **+73.6%** |
| Browse→cart | **4.556%** | 4.065% | **3.076%** | −1.48pp (**−32.5%**) |
| Cart→reached checkout* | 92.45% | 106.03% | 81.75% | −10.7pp (−11.6%) |
| Checkout completion | **39.40%** | **32.96%** | **35.78%** | −3.6pp (−9.2%) |
| Overall conversion | 1.660% | 1.421% | 0.900% | −0.76pp (**−45.8%**) |
| Completed checkouts | 8,694 | 10,833 | 8,180 | **−5.9%** |

The decisive line is checkout completion: **39.40% → 32.96% → 35.78%**. It bottomed in 2025 and has *recovered* 2.8pp into 2026. Meanwhile browse→cart declined every year, with the sharpest step in 2026 (−24.3% vs 2025). Checkout is healing; the front of the funnel is not.

**Contribution to the −45.8% conversion decline** (log decomposition, exactly additive):

| Stage | Share of the decline |
|---|---|
| Browse→cart | **64%** |
| Cart→reached checkout | 20% |
| Checkout completion | **16%** |

At the 2024 conversion rate, 909,372 sessions should have produced ~15,100 completed checkouts. You got 8,180 — a gap of **~6,900 orders**. Roughly 4,900 of those trace to the cart-add step; ~810 to checkout completion.

### Device: mobile is where it breaks

| Device | Sessions 2024→2026 | Browse→cart | Checkout completion | Overall CR |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mobile | 409,057 → 749,364 (+83%) | 4.33% → **2.79%** (−36%) | 38.82% → 36.56% (−5.8%) | 1.538% → 0.835% |
| Desktop | 94,883 → 134,958 (+42%) | 5.57% → 4.76% (−15%) | 40.78% → **32.74%** (−20%) | 2.202% → 1.279% |
| Tablet | 10,305 → 22,277 (+116%) | 3.97% → 2.78% | 40.69% → 41.61% | 1.378% → 0.880% |
| Other | 9,585 → 2,767 | 4.67% → 0.29% | 44.22% → **0%** (0 orders) | 1.794% → 0% |

Mobile absorbed **88% of the session growth** and its cart rate fell 36%. I tested whether the shift toward mobile explains the drop — it doesn't: holding 2026's per-device rates at 2024's device mix gives 0.901% vs the actual 0.900%. **Mix is worth ~0.002pp; essentially all of the decline is per-device rate decay.**

One genuine checkout finding: **desktop checkout completion fell 20%** (40.8%→32.7%, ~423 lost orders). That's a real defect worth chasing, but desktop is 15% of sessions, so it's ~6% of the total gap — not the headline.

### Where the traffic actually sits (last 30 days, 2026-06-30..07-30)

Window: 196,738 sessions, 3.12% cart rate, 35.7% checkout completion, 1.026% overall. Top 15 pages = 87% of sessions.

| Landing page | Sessions | Browse→cart | Checkout completion | Overall CR |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| /products/pet-monitor | 41,276 | 2.71% | 41.5% | 0.83% |
| /products/rv-4g-mini-camera | 32,231 | 2.42% | 39.4% | 0.69% |
| / | 18,778 | 5.25% | 41.6% | 1.85% |
| /pages/choose-your-bundle | 12,569 | 1.64% | **26.5%** | 0.41% |
| /blogs/heat-stroke-in-dogs/dog-panting-heat-stroke | 11,750 | **0.009%** | — | **0.00%** |
| /products/rv-4g-scout-bundle-pro-1 | 7,519 | 1.53% | 41.4% | 0.61% |
| /pages/exclusive-deals | 7,195 | **10.52%** | 36.9% | **3.70%** |
| /pages/waggle-home | 6,558 | 5.08% | 43.1% | 2.00% |
| /products/rv-backup-camera | 6,304 | 2.09% | 40.0% | 0.54% |
| /pages/choose-the-bundle | 5,886 | 1.85% | **19.4%** | 0.36% |
| /pages/store | 5,480 | 3.49% | 43.5% | 1.33% |

Three localized pools:

1. **The two dominant PDPs are the whole problem by volume.** `pet-monitor` + `rv-4g-mini-camera` = 73,507 sessions (37% of the window) at 2.71% and 2.42% cart rate — roughly half the homepage's 5.25%. Their checkout completion is *fine* (39–42%). People land and leave without adding. This is where the recoverable volume is.
2. **The blog page is dead weight.** 11,750 sessions (6% of traffic), **1 cart addition, 0 orders.** Either it has no product path wired in, or it isn't commercial traffic at all. It alone suppresses the site-wide cart rate by ~0.20pp (3.12% → 3.32% with it excluded).
3. **The bundle pages are a genuine checkout defect.** `choose-your-bundle` and `choose-the-bundle` — near-duplicate paths, 18,455 combined sessions (9.4%) — push people from cart to checkout at 95–99%, then lose them: **26.5% and 19.4% completion** vs 35.7% window average. This is the one place your checkout instinct is right. Also flag the duplication itself: `/pages/choose-your-bundle` vs `/pages/choose-the-bundle`, plus `/` vs `/pages/waggle-home` and `-b` variants on two PDPs — worth confirming these are intentional tests, not split traffic.

### Data quality and limits on this read

- **Sessions include bots — I could not separate them.** The tool rewrote my `human_or_bot_session = 'human'` filter back to `IN ('human','bot')`, which includes bot sessions. This is a material caveat: sessions +74% with a collapsing cart rate is exactly the signature of crawler inflation, so some part of the browse→cart decline may be a measurement artifact rather than a behavior change. I can't resolve it with the available data. `online_store_visitors` was also silently dropped from the query.
- **Reconciliation gap (not corrected):** the 2026 device-grain total is 909,380 sessions vs 909,372 in the aggregate — 8 sessions (0.0009%). 2024 matches exactly. Left as-is.
- **Cart→checkout is not strictly nested.** 2025 shows 32,868 sessions reaching checkout on 31,000 cart-add sessions (106%), so sessions can reach checkout without a recorded cart addition — Buy-now buttons, direct checkout or subscription links. Treat that middle term as an arithmetic bridge, not a drop-off rate. The two rates the business defines (browse→cart, checkout completion) are unaffected.
- **Window lengths differ slightly:** 213 / 212 / 211 days. On a per-day basis sessions are +75.3% and completed checkouts −5.0%; rate comparisons are unaffected.
- **Cuts I could not get:** referrer/UTM source, monthly timeseries, and the sales/orders dataset all got rewritten to the device breakdown, so I have no channel attribution for the +386K sessions, no date for a step change, and no independent order or revenue reconciliation. Landing pages exist only for the 30-day window, so page-level rates can't be compared to 2024.

**The channel attribution is the gap that matters most.** The finding that per-device rates decayed while mix stayed flat means the composition of traffic changed *within* mobile — and where those 386K extra sessions came from would tell you whether this is a paid-traffic-quality problem, an SEO-content problem, or bots. Worth pulling as soon as referrer/UTM data is available.

## Why your cells read high

Four distinct inflation sources, biggest first:

1. **Waving Girl — $60 of pure phantom budget.** `MLD-WavingGirl-2026_Traffic` is ACTIVE with a $60/day CBO, but its only two ad sets (`...FoodieInterest_21+_Feed`, `..._StoryReel`) are both PAUSED. Campaign-level `effective_status` says ACTIVE; nothing underneath can serve. This row is $0, not $60.
2. **Sauna House — $85 double count.** `MLD-RAL-Traffic-Pre_Sale` is PAUSED, but its paused ad set still carries an $85/day figure. Any reading that sums campaign *and* ad set budgets, or that ignores campaign status, shows up to $132 for a row that spends $47.
3. **Wildwood Reach — $16 on a paused campaign.** `MLD-WildWood-2026-Reach` holds a live-looking $16/day CBO while both it and its child are paused.
4. **~$1,454 of dead lifetime budget.** The two `All_Locations` boosted-post campaigns are paused, and their 20 ad sets hold lifetime budgets ($1,029 + $425) with end dates between Feb 13 and May 30 — all in the past. If anything prorates lifetime into a daily column, this dwarfs everything real in the account.

Summing every budget field visible across these accounts gives roughly **$548/day against $387 of real capacity** — about 29% overstated, and the error is concentrated entirely in Meta.

## Two structural notes

- **The `All_Locations` newsletter campaign is ABO and splits across four properties.** Its $8/day lives in four $2 ad sets named per property (Nuvole, Capella, Wildwood, Urban Oak) — no Bargello or Waving Girl adset exists. Rolling that campaign up as one line would misattribute $8 and leave two rows short. Also, both ABO campaigns report campaign `budget_remaining: 0`, which is normal for ABO and not a sign of exhaustion.
- **$14/day is genuinely unattributable.** Campaign `120246759443390194` ("Instagram post: Girl Dinner just got a room…", $14/day CBO, runs through 2026-06-19) has no property token in its name, no `promoted_object.page_id`, and Instagram-profile targeting only. It's real spendable money in the McKibbon account but I won't assign it to a property on a guess — I've held it as a separate line. The ad's creative would settle it; ad data isn't in this dataset.

Two caveats on how far I could verify: Meta stops at ad-set level, so an active ad set with all ads paused would push a row *lower* — never higher. On Google, the connector didn't return `serving_status`, `end_date`, or `explicitly_shared`, so those two rows are verified at campaign+budget level only; I did confirm all 8 budget resource IDs are distinct, so there's no shared-budget double count to strip in either account.

## Caveats

- **Google upper bound:** $262 is the configured daily rate. Google can deliver up to 2× a daily budget on any single day (bounded by the 30.4× monthly cap), so a single-day Google ceiling is nearer $524 even though the sustained rate is $262.

## Two caveats on method

**Google serving eligibility is unverified.** `campaign.end_date`, `serving_status`, `primary_status`, `campaign_budget.period`, and `explicitly_shared` all come back empty from this environment regardless of how I query them. So Google's $262 assumes the 8 enabled campaigns are within flight and on daily (not custom-period) budgets. If any has a lapsed end date, the real number is lower — that's the one gap I couldn't close.

**I tested and rejected an ad-level filter.** An empty ad list initially looked like it proved the $14 Girl Dinner campaign couldn't deliver, which would have zeroed it. But `facebook_list_ads` returns empty for *both accounts entirely*, including ad sets that are visibly spending today (Wildwood reservations shows $8.38 consumed of $10.00). The endpoint is unpopulated here, not evidence of missing ads — so "active campaign + active ad set + in-schedule" is the deepest test the data actually supports. ## Caveats finance needs before treating any of this as an audit trail

1. **Conversions are fractional, not events.** Values like 0.5, 1.5, 0.989272, 0.992118, 0.994172, 0.999386 appear — attributed credit split across touchpoints. Even the 61% that *does* have a search term will not map 1:1 to lead records.
2. **151 search‑term rows ≠ 151 distinct searches.** The same term recurs across ad groups ("headhunter" in 3, "agência de emprego novo hamburgo" in 3, plus "page personnel", "meuemprego", "emprego gravatai", "produtive carreira", "consultoria linkedin", "headhunters"). Row counts are not user counts.
3. **The window is still moving.** Conversions stamp to interaction date and the snapshot (2026-06-24T22:16Z) is one day past window close, so late arrivals will restate 290.97 upward. Treat as provisional.
4. **The 2.0 keyword‑less residual can't be diagnosed further with this data.** Conversion‑action and ad‑network segmentation both returned campaign totals with the segment column absent, and `segments.keyword.info.text` came back empty — so the term→keyword bridge had to be built via ad group rather than directly. The usual suspects (Search Partners, a conversion action with no keyword lineage) are plausible but unverified here; I'd flag it rather than assert it.
5. **The 39% gap is structural, not a tracking defect.** Google withholds search terms searched by too few users. No configuration change recovers it — so a search‑level audit trail for the full reported figure is not obtainable, and any finance control requiring one should be re‑scoped to keyword grain (99.3% coverage).

## Short answer: no — and two different reasons, only one of which is Google's fault

**Campaign control (Search, 2026-05-25 → 06-23): 290.974948 conversions, 2,942 clicks.** Confirmed twice — `campaign` and `campaign_search_term_insight` returned identical totals.

| Grain | Conversions | % of control | Clicks | % of control |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Campaign (control) | 290.974948 | 100% | 2,942 | 100% |
| Keyword (`keyword_view`) | 288.974948 | **99.31%** | 2,890 | 98.2% |
| Search term (`search_term_view`) | 177.474948 | **60.99%** | 445 | **15.1%** |

### 1. Keyword side: 2.00 conversions tie to nothing
SP and BH reconcile **exactly**. The entire residual is 2.000000 conversions in `[PB][PDT] SEARCH | LANDBOT | ONGOING | RS` (21111476111). I checked one level down — the ad-group grain also totals 288.974948, the same gap — so those 2 conversions aren't resolvable to any ad group either. Finance should carry them as a named campaign-level exception, not chase them.

### 2. "Currently enabled" cannot be evidenced at all
This half of your question is **unanswerable with this connector**, and I'd rather say so than pass along a green tick. All 34 keyword rows report `status: ENABLED`, but that value is worthless here:

- `WHERE ad_group_criterion.status != 'ENABLED'` returned **all 34 ENABLED rows** — the predicate was silently ignored. (Control test: `WHERE campaign.id = ...` correctly returned only BH's 9 rows, so filtering works generally — it's the status predicate specifically.)
- Querying `ad_group_criterion` directly **dropped** `status`, `criterion_id`, `type`, `negative`, and `ad_group.status` from the response entirely.
- That same query returned exactly the 34 keywords that converted — zero non-converting keywords, zero negatives. So it isn't the criterion inventory; the account's current keyword list can't be enumerated.

So: 99.3% of conversions tie to a *named* keyword. Whether those keywords are *currently enabled* is unverified, and a paused-keyword conversion would be indistinguishable from an enabled one in this data.

### 3. The 61% search-term figure is flattering, not reassuring
The 113.5-conversion gap is real, but I can't tell you it's Google's normal low-volume/privacy suppression, because the table itself is partial:

- `WHERE metrics.conversions = 0` → **0 rows**. Ordering by impressions instead of conversions still yields only converting terms.
- Not one non-converting search term exists in the dataset — impossible for a live account with 60,160 impressions.
- Hence the split: 61% of *conversions* but only **15.1% of clicks** are covered. 2,497 clicks have no search term at all.

Clicks are observed, never modeled — so that 15.1% is the honest ceiling on how much spend you can trace to a real user query here, and the 61% is an artifact of only converting terms being present.

### 4. Some of it can never be tied to a search, by construction
The data contains fractional conversions: `70.988658`, `45.98629`, `1.5`, `0.999386`, `0.994172`, `0.992118`, `0.989272`, and several `0.5`. These are modeled/cross-device or fractionally-attributed values. A 0.5 conversion has no single originating query — no amount of data access fixes that. Any finance process demanding 1:1 conversion-to-search mapping will fail on these permanently.

### Grain-overlap warning (per your brief)
`search_term_view` rows are per keyword×term, so terms repeat: `agência de emprego novo hamburgo` appears **3×** in RS (1, 6, 6 clicks), `emprego gravatai` 2× in RS, `headhunter` in all three campaigns, `consultoria linkedin` in both SP and BH. Never sum keyword and search-term grains together, and de-dup before any term-level rollup.

## What I'd tell finance
Use **290.97 at campaign grain** as the auditable number. Present keyword grain as 99.31% reconciled with one 2.00 exception in RS. **Do not** present the search-term grain as a completeness statement — it would imply 39% is unexplained when the true traceable share is nearer 15%, and neither number is a clean audit result.

Two gaps are worth closing before the next cycle, both connector-side rather than account-side: keyword status/inventory fields, and non-converting search terms. Also note `segments.date` is unavailable, so with the snapshot falling one day after window close (2026-06-24T22:16Z), some conversion settling is likely but I could not quantify it.

### One finding that changes how you read the 39%
The search-term extract for this window contains **only converting terms** — all 151 rows have ≥0.5 conversions, and ordering by clicks descending surfaces no zero-conversion rows at all. It covers 61.0% of conversions but only **15.1% of clicks** (445 of 2,942). Google's low-volume/privacy threshold suppresses terms by *query volume* regardless of outcome; it would not preferentially retain the converting ones. That asymmetry means the extract is conversion-filtered, so I cannot split the 113.50 into "Google withheld it" versus "the extract dropped it." Treat 39.0% as a **floor** on what is unidentifiable to a search query, not a measured privacy-withholding rate.

The conversion counts are fractionally credited (70.988658, 1.5, 0.999386, 0.5), i.e. data-driven attribution. Even the 99.3% that ties to a keyword does not decompose into whole discrete user actions — so "every reported conversion" can't be made to reconcile to a countable list of events at any grain.

- Status predicates (`ad_group_criterion.status != 'ENABLED'`) were ignored and returned the same ENABLED rows. - `segments.date` was dropped from results, and `change_event` / `campaign_search_term_insight` returned no usable fields — so I could not localize the 2.000 to a date or to an audit event.

Both responses returned `response_complete: true` / `paging_next_present: false`, so neither is a partial page. A three-metric exact tie across two independently-queried grains is the corroboration your finance requirement asks for.

## The four totals

| Source | Spend | Rows | vs. account |
|---|---|---|---|
| Account total | **61,197.95** | 1 | — |
